--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score increases by 1 for every robot outside the target region (BLACK floor strip bordered by BLUE walls)
- Robots need to navigate to the BLACK floor strip near BLUE walls
- Key perceptions: floor color (BLACK = target strip or other strip, GRAY = middle), colored objects (BLUE walls = target, GREEN walls = wrong strip)

**Strategy:**
- Robots on BLACK floor near BLUE walls: good (score 0 contribution)
- Robots on BLACK floor near GREEN walls: bad (should leave)
- Robots on GRAY floor: transitioning, should move toward BLUE walls
- Key insight: Use GoToColor with blue (clr=3) to head toward BLUE walls, and GoAwayColor with green (clr=2) to avoid GREEN walls

**State Design:**
- State 0: GoToColor blue (clr=3) - move toward blue walls
- State 1: Exploration - wander when no blue visible
- State 2: Stop - when on black floor near blue walls

**Floor colors:**
- BLACK (C=0): robot is in a strip (target or wrong)
- GRAY (C=1): robot is in the middle
- Seeing BLUE walls (C=7, l=3): near target
- Seeing GREEN walls (C=7, l=2): near wrong strip

**Transitions logic:**
- If seeing blue → go to blue
- If on black + seeing blue → stop (we're there)
- If on black + seeing green → go away or explore

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Best candidate (elitism placeholder)**: 2-state: GoToColor(blue) with transition to stop on black+blue visible
2. Various topologies exploring the space

**Color codes**: clr=3 is blue, clr=2 is green, clr=1 is red
Wait - colors for walls: BLUE walls border target strip. The color IDs: 1=red,2=green,3=blue,4=yellow,5=magenta,6=cyan. So blue=3, green=2.

Let me design the machines:

FSM: `--nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 3 --n0 1 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 3 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --l1x0 2 --p1x0 0.8`

This: GoToBlue → if black floor (0.9) → Stop; Stop → if sees green (wrong side) → GoToBlue

Let me write all 14:

## Reasoning

**Fixing errors first:**
- LED colors (`--cle`) must be one of {0, 4, 5, 6} — NOT 1,2,3. So I cannot use 3 (blue). I'll use 0 (off), 4 (yellow), 5 (magenta), or 6 (cyan).

**Mission recap:**
- Minimize time robots spend outside the BLACK strip near BLUE walls
- GoToColor with clr=3 (blue) moves toward blue walls → target
- GoAwayColor with clr=2 (green) moves away from green walls → also toward target
- Floor BLACK (C=0) = in a strip (target or wrong), GRAY (C=1) = middle
- Seeing blue (C=7, l=3) = near target, seeing green (C=7, l=2) = near wrong strip

**Core strategy:**
- Primary: GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) to head toward blue walls
- If on black floor AND seeing green → wrong strip, need to escape
- If on black floor AND seeing blue → stop, we're at target
- Use GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2) to escape wrong strip

**14 machines with corrected LED values:**

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The errors persist - I keep using cle=3 which is invalid. Valid LED colors are ONLY {0, 4, 5, 6}. Let me be very careful: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. I will NOT use 1, 2, or 3 for any `--cle` parameter.

**Strategy recap:**
- GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3): moves toward blue walls = target strip
- Stop (B=1): stay in place when at target
- GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2): flee from wrong strip
- Exploration (B=0): wander when lost
- Key transitions: floor BLACK (C=0) = in a strip, floor GRAY (C=1) = middle, see blue walls (C=7, l=3), see green walls (C=7, l=2)

**Core FSM logic:**
- State 0: GoToColor(blue) — if black floor → stop; if see green → go away green
- State 1: Stop — if see green → flee; if gray floor → go back to GoToColor
- State 2: GoAwayColor(green) — if see blue → GoToColor; if gray floor → GoToColor

I will use cle values only from {0, 4, 5, 6}.

## Reasoning

The error message is very clear and I keep making the same mistake. Let me audit every single `--cle` value before writing. Valid values: **0, 4, 5, 6 ONLY**. I will NEVER write `--cle? 3`, `--cle? 1`, or `--cle? 2`.

Note: `--clr` (color for GoToColor/GoAwayColor behaviour) CAN be 3 (blue) or 2 (green) - that's a different parameter. Only `--cle` (LED color) is restricted to {0,4,5,6}.

**Strategy:**
- B=8 GoToColor with clr=3 (blue walls = target): primary navigation
- B=1 Stop: once at target (black floor, no green walls visible)
- B=9 GoAwayColor with clr=2 (green walls = wrong strip): escape wrong strip
- Transitions: C=0 (black floor), C=1 (gray floor), C=7 (see colored object)

## Reasoning

Looking at my FSM line: `--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 3 --cle1 6`

Wait - `--clr1 3` means GoToColor targeting color 3 (blue). That's fine. But `--cle1 6` should be valid. 

Oh wait - I see the issue! When I write `--clr0 3`, the system might be parsing `--cle` parameters incorrectly, OR the error message is about something I'm not seeing. Let me look more carefully...

Actually, looking at the error: it says `--cle0`, `--cle1`, `--cle2` used invalid value '3'. But in my machines I write `--clr0 3` (behaviour color parameter) and `--cle0 6` (LED color). 

Could the parser be confusing `--clr` with `--cle`? No, they're different strings.

Wait - could the issue be that `--cle` must come BEFORE behaviour-specific params? Let me reorder: put `--cle` right after the state behaviour ID.

Actually, re-reading more carefully: maybe some machines DO have cle=3 that I missed. Let me just write everything fresh and super carefully, putting cle values first after behavior declaration, using ONLY {0, 4, 5, 6}.

## Reasoning

I have multiple errors to fix. The persistent `--cle` error with value '3' is baffling since I'm writing `--cle0 6`. Let me look very carefully at one of my FSMs from round 7:

`--nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 30 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 3 --p0x0 0.8 --n0x1 1 --c0x1 0 --p0x0 0.3`

Wait! I see it: `--p0x0 0.3` appears TWICE (I accidentally reused `--p0x0` instead of `--p0x1`). This might confuse the parser.

Also new errors: `--n0 ...` not integer, missing destination index, missing `--n1`. These suggest the parser is getting confused by malformed earlier parameters and failing to parse the rest correctly.

Let me write very simple, clean FSMs with no mistakes:

**Key rules to follow:**
1. `--cle<I>` only values: 0, 4, 5, 6
2. No `--clr` parameter (to avoid any confusion)
3. Every `--n<I>x<K>` must have destination D
4. Every transition needs condition C and its parameters
5. `--p` for C=7 is probability (real), `--l` is the color integer

I'll write minimal 2-state machines first, then build up.

**Simple 2-state design:**
- S0: Exploration, cle=0. 2 transitions: →S1 if see blue (C=7,l=3,p=0.8); →S1 if black floor (C=0,p=0.5)
- S1: Stop, cle=6. 2 transitions: →S0 if see green (C=7,l=2,p=0.9); →S0 if gray floor (C=1,p=0.5)

Let me write this out character by character:

`--nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 30 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 3 --p0x0 0.8 --n0x1 1 --c0x1 0 --p0x1 0.5 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --l1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9 --n1x1 0 --c1x1 1 --p1x1 0.5`

Check: cle0=0 ✓, cle1=0 ✓. n0=2 (integer) ✓. n0x0 destination=1 ✓. n0x1 destination=1 ✓. n1=2 ✓. n1x0 destination=0 ✓. n1x1 destination=0 ✓.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same errors keep appearing. I need to think very carefully about what's going wrong. The errors say:
1. `--cle` values using 3 (but I'm writing 0, 4, 5, 6!)
2. `--n0` not an integer
3. Missing destination index for `--n0x0`
4. Missing `--n1`
5. Missing destination for `--n1x0`

These cascading errors suggest the parser is failing early and then everything downstream breaks. The root cause must be something early in the command line that corrupts parsing.

Wait - could the problem be that I'm not including `--nstates` at all? The error says "Missing or invalid '--nstates'". But I DO write `--nstates 2` etc. Unless... the FSM line itself has a formatting issue.

Let me look at whether the `FSM: ` prefix is being included correctly. The instruction says "each machine on its own line starting with 'FSM: '". Maybe the colon or space is causing issues in my output format.

Actually, I think the real problem might be something completely different: **the `--l` parameter for C=7 conditions**. When I write `--l0x0 3`, the value `3` comes right before things like `--p0x0`. Could the system be reading `--l0x0 3` and somehow associating `3` with `--cle`?

No, that doesn't make sense syntactically.

Let me try a radically different approach: **write the absolute simplest possible FSMs** with only 1 state and 1 transition, then build up slowly. Also, let me try putting `--nstates` in a different position, and avoid the number 3 entirely as a value where possible.

Actually - I just realized something. The `--l` parameter for condition C=7: `--l<I>x<K>`. If I'm looking for blue walls, that's color 3. So I write `--l0x0 3`. Could the validator be checking this as if it's a `--cle` value? That would be a bug in the validator, but maybe that's what's happening.

**Test hypothesis**: avoid using `l=3` (blue color) in C=7 conditions. Instead, use `l=2` (green) only to detect and flee from wrong strip. 

But then how do robots find the blue strip? They could use floor color: BLACK floor means they're in a strip. If they're on black floor and DON'T see green walls, they're probably at the target. So:
- Explore until black floor
- If black floor, stop
- If stop and see green (wrong strip), explore again

This avoids `--l?x? 3` entirely!

Let me try this approach with very simple FSMs:

**Machine 1**: 2 states
- S0: Exploration (B=0, rwm=30), cle=0
  - →S1 if black floor (C=0, p=0.8)
- S1: Stop (B=1), cle=6
  - →S0 if see green walls (C=7, l=2, p=0.9)
  - →S0 if gray floor (C=1, p=0.5)

No `l=3` anywhere! Let me write this carefully:

`--nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 30 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.8 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --l1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9 --n1x1 0 --c1x1 1 --p1x1 0.5`

Check each element:
- `--nstates 2` ✓
- `--s0 0` behavior 0 (Exploration) ✓
- `--rwm0 30` rwm integer 30 ✓
- `--cle0 0` LED off, value 0 ✓
- `--n0 1` one transition ✓
- `--n0x0 1` destination = state 1 ✓
- `--c0x0 0` condition BLACK floor ✓
- `--p0x0 0.8` probability ✓
- `--s1 1` behavior 1 (Stop) ✓
- `--cle1 6` LED cyan, value 6 ✓
- `--n1 2` two transitions ✓
- `--n1x0 0` destination = state 0 ✓
- `--c1x0 7` condition: see color ✓
- `--l1x0 2` color = green ✓
- `--p1x0 0.9` probability ✓
- `--n1x1 0` destination = state 0 ✓
- `--c1x1 1` condition: gray floor ✓
- `--p1x1 0.5` probability ✓

This looks correct. No value of 3 anywhere except... nowhere! Let me write all 13 this way.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same errors keep appearing round after round. I need to fundamentally rethink what's going wrong.

The errors say `--cle0` has value `3`, but I write `--cle0 0` or `--cle0 6`. This is impossible UNLESS the FSM line itself is not starting correctly, and the parser is reading a different token as `--cle0`.

**Critical insight**: The error says "Missing or invalid '--nstates'" - this means the parser isn't finding `--nstates` at all! If `--nstates` is missing, the whole parse fails and everything cascades. 

But I write `--nstates 2` right at the start! Unless... the `FSM: ` prefix is being included in the argument string and the parser sees `FSM:` as the first token, not `--nstates`.

**The real bug**: Maybe the system strips `FSM: ` and reads from there, but if there's any leading whitespace or character issue, it fails. OR maybe the system is reading my reasoning text as part of the FSM line!

Let me look at my format. I write:
```
FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 ...
```

That should be fine. But what if the issue is that the system reads the ENTIRE LINE after "FSM: " and my line wraps or has special characters?

Actually, re-reading the error more carefully: it says the SAME errors every round. Maybe these errors are from a SUBSET of machines (the bad ones get rejected, good ones run). The valid machines DO run, and the optimizer IS working, but I keep submitting some bad ones.

Let me count: if I submit 13 machines and some fail validation, only the valid ones run. The optimizer might be getting very few valid machines each round.

The question is: which of my machines fail? Let me look for any pattern...

In round 9, I wrote FSMs that avoid `l=3` in conditions. The cle errors persist. Let me look at what value `3` could appear as cle...

OH WAIT. I just realized: `--s0 0 --rwm0 30 --cle0 0` - what if `--rwm0` is being parsed as a cle parameter? No, that makes no sense.

What if the issue is `--c0x0 0`? Condition type 0 = black floor. Then `--p0x0 0.8`. But what if the parser sees `--c0x0` and then `0` and then `--p0x0` and `0.8` fine...

Actually let me try a COMPLETELY different hypothesis: **the errors reported are from PREVIOUS rounds' machines that are still in the population**, not my new machines. The system keeps old machines and the errors are from machines I submitted in rounds 1-3 (which had `--cle? 3`). My new machines might be fine!

If that's the case, I should just keep submitting good machines and the old bad ones will eventually be replaced.

But the budget is decreasing, suggesting machines ARE running. So some are valid.

Let me try the most minimal possible FSMs to ensure validity:

**1-state machine** (just explore forever):
`--nstates 1 --s0 0 --rwm0 30 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 5 --p0x0 0.001`

Wait, with 1 state, `--n0x0 D` - D must index the OTHER states. With 1 state, there are NO other states. So 1-state machines can't have transitions? Or they must point to themselves? The rules say "Never point a transition at its own state." So with 1 state you can have 0 transitions? But `--n0` must be in [1..4].

Actually with 1 state, can we have transitions? The destination D "indexed among the OTHER states only" - with 1 state there are no others. So maybe 1-state machines must have 0 transitions, but `--n<I>` requires [1..4]. Contradiction. So minimum is 2 states.

Let me write the absolute simplest valid 2-state machine and verify every token:

Token by token:
`--nstates` `2` `--s0` `0` `--rwm0` `30` `--cle0` `0` `--n0` `1` `--n0x0` `1` `--c0x0` `0` `--p0x0` `0.8` `--s1` `1` `--cle1` `6` `--n1` `1` `--n1x0` `0` `--c1x0` `1` `--p1x0` `0.5`

- `--nstates 2`: 2 states ✓
- `--s0 0`: state 0 = Exploration ✓ needs `--rwm0`
- `--rwm0 30`: turning rate 30 ✓
- `--cle0 0`: LED off ✓ (0 is valid)
- `--n0 1`: 1 transition from state 0 ✓
- `--n0x0 1`: transition 0 of state 0 goes to state 1 ✓ (with 2 states, leaving state 0, others=[1], D=0 targets state 1... wait)

**WAIT**. Re-reading the spec: "With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0."

So `--n0x0 D` where D=0 means "the first (and only) other state" = state 1! D is NOT the state index, it's an INDEX INTO THE LIST OF OTHER STATES!

So with 2 states:
- From state 0: other states = [1], so D=0 → goes to state 1
- From state 1: other states = [0], so D=0 → goes to state 0

With 3 states:
- From state 0: others = [1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: others = [0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- From state 2: others = [0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1

With 4 states:
- From state 0: others = [1,2,3], D=0→1, D=1→2, D=2→3
- From state 1: others = [0,2,3], D=0→0, D=1→2, D=2→3
- From state 2: others = [0,1,3], D=0→0, D=1→1, D=2→3
- From state 3: others = [0,1,2], D=0→0, D=1→1, D=2→2

**THIS IS THE BUG I'VE BEEN MISSING ALL ALONG!**

In my machines, I've been writing `--n0x0 1` meaning "go to state 1" but the spec says D=0 means "first other state" (=state 1 when leaving state 0). So I should write `--n0x0 0` not `--n0x0 1`!

When I write `--n0x0 1` with 2 states, the system expects D in [0..0] (only one other state), so D=1 is OUT OF RANGE, causing parse errors!

And similarly, `--n1x0 0` from state 1 with 2 states: other states = [0], D=0 → state 0. That's correct.

But `--n0x0 1` from state 0 with 2 states: D must be 0 (pointing to the only other state = state 1). Writing D=1 is invalid!

Let me re-examine my 3-state machines:
- From state 0, others=[1,2]: `--n0x0 1` means D=1 → state 2. `--n0x0 0` → state 1. 
- I was writing `--n0x0 1` and `--n0x1 2` which would mean D=1→state2 and D=2→OUT OF RANGE!

This explains ALL the errors! The parser sees invalid destination indices and then fails to parse the rest of the line correctly.

**Now let me recalibrate all destination indices:**

**2 states:**
- From S0: D=0 → S1 (only option)
- From S1: D=0 → S0 (only option)

**3 states:**
- From S0: D=0→S1, D=1→S2
- From S1: D=0→S0, D=1→S2
- From S2: D=0→S0, D=1→S1

**4 states:**
- From S0: D=0→S1, D=1→S2, D=2→S3
- From S1: D=0→S0, D=1→S2, D=2→S3
- From S2: D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S3
- From S3: D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S2

Now let me redesign all machines with correct D values. And `--cle` only {0,4,5,6}.

**Strategy** (using floor color since l=3 might cause issues - actually l=3 should be fine as it's `--l` not `--cle`):
- Explore until on black floor → stop
- If stopped and see green → explore again (wrong strip)
- Multiple variations

Machine 1 (2 states, simple):
- S0: Exploration(rwm=30), cle=0. n=1. Transition: D=0(→S1), C=0(black), p=0.8
- S1: Stop, cle=6. n=2. T0: D=0(→S0), C=7(see green,l=2), p=0.9. T1: D=0(→S0), C=1(gray), p=0.5

Machine 2 (2 states):
- S0: Exploration(rwm=20), cle=0. n=2. T0: D=0(→S1), C=0, p=0.9. T1: D=0(→S1), C=5, p=0.01
- S1: Stop, cle=6. n=2. T0: D=0(→S0), C=7(l=2), p=0.9. T1: D=0(→S0), C=1, p=0.4

Machine 3 (3 states):
- S0: Exploration(rwm=25), cle=0. n=2. T0: D=0(→S1), C=0, p=0.85. T1: D=1(→S2), C=7(l=2), p=0.7
- S1: Stop, cle=6. n=2. T0: D=0(→S0), C=7(l=2), p=0.9. T1: D=1(→S2), C=1, p=0.4
- S2: Exploration(rwm=60), cle=4. n=2. T0: D=1(→S1), C=0, p=0.8. T1: D=0(→S0), C=5, p=0.05

Let me now write all 12 machines carefully:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 30 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 0.8 --s1 1 --cle1 6 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --l1x0 2 --p1x0 0.9 --n1x1 0 --c1x1 1 --p1x1 0.5

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
